Among them, "Kongai Thirangi" is compiled as the first Thiruppathikam, and the Pathikam beginning with "Etti Ilavam" is compiled as the second Thiruppathikam. Here, as mentioned by Sekkizhar Peruman, the Mootha Thiruppathikam beginning with "Kongai Thirangi," now sung by Ammaiyaar, is set to the tune of Nattapadai. "With shrivelled breasts, veins standing out, deep-set eyes, white teeth, a swollen belly, red lips, long protruding teeth, and long, high-heeled legs, a female ghoul, shrieking and drying up in the forest, with matted locks spread in eight directions, our Appan, whose body is cool, dances in the fire. His abode is ThiruvAlangadu." This is the first verse. Thus, Ammaiyaar sings the tenth concluding verse as: "The one without adornment, when the ghouls gather, striking each other, singing in a discordant manner, and the Lord dances, the Karaikal ghoul, who is like a tender sprout, sang these ten beautiful Tamil verses about the Lord of ThiruvAlangadu, who is our Appan, and those who master them will attain Shivagati and experience bliss." With this tenth verse, Ammaiyaar concludes this Thiruppathikam. According to the divine command received in Thirukailai, Ammaiyaar reached ThiruvAlangadu by walking on her head, to witness the sacred dance and sing at His feet. She worshipped that site, which was a great forest filled with banyan trees. There, she saw many ghouls singing, dancing, and playing musical instruments with joy. Thus, the Lord showed His sacred dance, where He raised His foot to the heavens, to Ammaiyaar, in the presence of all these ghouls. The place where this was seen is ThiruvAlangadu, and Ammaiyaar composed this Thiruppathikam, praising the site. It is characteristic of Ammaiyaar's Pathikams to praise the site by focusing on the sacred dance performed by the Lord. Worshipping and praising a site is one of the methods of worship. Sekkizhar Peruman also instructs us on this idea, saying, "When seeing the form of Him dancing in Alangadu, sing and praise the dance." Sekkizhar Peruman then continues to sing about the second Mootha Thiruppathikam composed by Ammaiyaar. Ammaiyaar, who had worshipped the sacred dance of Lord Shiva, adorned with fragrant Konrai flower garlands, with ever-increasing love and overflowing desire, then composed another Thiruppathikam, consisting of complete verses, starting with "Etti Ilavam Eegai" and concluding with "The Lord dances while drums beat and ghouls sing." In this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the Lord as "Mattavizh Konraiyinar" (one adorned with fragrant Konrai). Konrai is the Lord's sacred garland. It is the form of Pranava. Similarly, the sacred dance performed by the Lord is also the form of Pranava. Therefore, by singing "Mattavizh Konraiyinardam Thirukkootthu" (the sacred dance of the Lord adorned with Konrai flowers), Sekkizhar Peruman implicitly conveys that it is the sacred dance of Lord Shiva. Ammaiyaar had a great desire to witness this sacred dance.

She had a great fondness for it. As her desire to worship this dance grew and overflowed, that love became immense, transforming into wonder. In that state of mind, considering the great blessing she received by worshipping the Lord's sacred dance, Ammaiyaar composed the second Thiruppathikam. Through her description in this Pathikam, where she portrays the Lord Shiva, the beautiful one, dancing while all the ghouls, including herself, beat drums and sing, we understand Ammaiyaar's state of mind. "Etti Ilavam Eegai" is the beginning of this second ThiruvAlangattu Mootha Thiruppathikam. This second Thiruppathikam is set to the tune of Indalappann. "In the forest where Etti, Ilavam, Eegai, Surai, Karai creep everywhere, surrounded by the cremation ground, with Kalli trees, where intestines are caught, and scattered corpses lie, the ghouls with drum-like eyes beat drums, and the beautiful Lord dances while ghouls sing." This is the first verse in this Thiruppathikam. Ammaiyaar continues to sing, and in the 11th concluding verse, she composes as follows: "By the grace of the Lord who has the cool moon on His matted locks, who dances the swirling sacred dance, and who has a serpent tied around His waist, the Karaikal ghoul, with fiery fangs, sang these ten verses, and by singing and dancing, sins are destroyed." With this concluding verse, Ammaiyaar completes this Thiruppathikam. Ammaiyaar composes that in ThiruvAlangadu, surrounded by ghouls, the Lord dances in rhythm, with music and instruments playing. Here, the Lord shows this sacred dance to Ammaiyaar, and Ammaiyaar sings about the state she witnessed, so that the people of the world may know it as it is. The Lord is the first cause, the cause of dissolution. Ammaiyaar is in the form of a ghoul, one of the Lord's sacred hosts. Therefore, Ammaiyaar sees the Lord dancing, surrounded by ghouls. The place where the Lord dances is the cremation ground. There are trees like Vagai, Kalli, Eegai, etc. Among the ghouls living there are mothers and children. Jackals also live there. Owls and other birds sing there. The red fire lit for the rituals of the deceased serves as the lamp there. The ghouls gather, run, dance, and sing with joy. They sing the seven notes of music. They play many musical instruments. Thus, in the hot cremation ground, our Appan, with a cool body, dances with grace. That place is ThiruvAlangadu. This is the essence of this Thiruppathikam. Sekkizhar Peruman then composes the next verse. "Sekkizhar Peruman composes the next verse, the 65th verse, saying, 'How can one describe the immense glory of Ammaiyaar, who was graced by the Lord with matted locks holding the overflowing Ganga, who called her with sweet words, "Ammai," and who always remains at the feet of His sacred raised foot in His glorious dance?'" Lord Shiva is the one who holds the overflowing Ganga in His matted locks. He held that great flood like a small dewdrop in His matted locks and contained it. Such a Lord Shiva called our Ammaiyaar, Karaikal Ammaiyaar, with His sweet and gentle words, "Ammaiye." Our Ammaiyaar, of such great glory, always remains at the feet of the Lord when He performs His sacred dance. The Lord's foot is the raised sacred foot. The Lord's foot is the one that lifts souls from the mire of anava (ego) and places them at His feet, bestowing bliss. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman sings in Thevaram about this as "Eduttha Porpadham" (the raised golden foot). The raised foot is different from the planted foot. According to the Unmai Vilakkam verse, "Seek Mukti at the raised lotus feet," the raised foot is the foot that bestows grace. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to it here as "Eduttharulum Sevadi" (the raised sacred foot that bestows grace). Here, the raised foot of the Lord is the foot that granted Ammaiyaar that irreversible state, that liberation. Our Ammaiyaar always remains at that sacred foot.

This state is the state that is equally attained by everyone who reaches the Lord's feet. However, this state attained by Ammaiyaar is special because she specifically requested, "I wish to be at Your feet when You dance." Thus, Ammaiyaar is one who attained great glory. She is worthy of the greatness of having received exactly what she asked for. Who can describe the greatness of Ammaiyaar in this way? It is beyond our capacity. The specialness of Ammaiyaar, that great specialness, is immeasurable, and I cannot describe it. Not only that, no one can describe it. Ammaiyaar's specialness is immeasurable. Ammaiyaar not only remains at the Lord's feet when He dances but also has the special privilege of singing. Sekkizhar Peruman says that no one can describe the great specialness of such an Ammaiyaar. Sekkizhar Peruman then composes the concluding verse, the 66th verse. "When the Lord, who has no beginning or end, performs His dance of grace, I praise the radiant lotus feet of Ammaiyaar, who sings before Him. Now I shall begin to praise the sacred service performed by Appoodhi Adigal, the great sage, in Thingoor, surrounded by cool water-filled fields." Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman concludes the Puranam of Ammaiyaar. The Lord's sacred dance is a dance of grace without beginning or end. Sekkizhar Peruman says in this verse, "When the Lord performs that dance of grace, I praise the radiant lotus-like feet of Ammaiyaar, who sings melodious songs at His feet." Sekkizhar Peruman concludes by emphasizing Ammaiyaar singing at the Lord's feet here. This is because this is the boon Ammaiyaar asked for and received. This boon encompasses all other boons. It is in this manner of singing that all other historical narratives, all other histories of Ammaiyaar, come to mind. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings and concludes this entire history by focusing on this. Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Ammaiyaar, who sings before Him." Music that has the Lord as its inner meaning is the music that can grant us salvation. All other music, all other songs, are empty music that are limited to sensory pleasure. But a song that has the Lord as its inner meaning, that music, will bring us salvation. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman, who concludes this sacred Puranam by using the name "Ammai" with which the Lord called her, praises Ammaiyaar's lotus feet, as she forever sings before the Lord. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman, praising and concluding the sacred Puranam of our Ammaiyaar, praises Ammaiyaar's feet and then states that he will now begin to narrate the sacred service performed by Appoodhi Adigal, the great sage, who lived in Thingoor. Thus, he sets the stage for the upcoming Puranam of Appoodhi Adigal Nayanar. We too, having happily concluded Ammaiyaar Puranam, will now continue with Appoodhi Adigal Nayanar Puranam, with the support of Ammaiyaar's feet. At this point, Sivakavimani C.K.S. Aiya has provided detailed commentaries on Ammaiyaar's ThiruvAlangattu Mootha Thiruppathikam One, Two, Thiru Irattai Manimalai, and Thiruvanthathi. Devotees can read and learn from them. With this information, we will continue to reflect on Appoodhi Adigal Nayanar Puranam. Praise, praise to the feet of Karaikal Ammaiyaar. Praise, praise to the feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Praise, praise to the feet of Nambiyandar Nambigal. Praise, praise to the feet of Sekkizhar Peruman. Praise, praise to the feet of Sivakavimani Aiya, who provided the great commentary on Periyapuranam. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.
